The uniform assertion (6.39) consequently puts all the contributing central conjugator classes in one compact subset of \(G/Z\). The absolute integrand is bounded on the resulting compact product. It is integrable, so Fubini applies.

Invert the conjugation variable, using unimodularity, in that ordered formula. The inner integral is \(\int_{G/Z}c_u(x^{-1}gx)d\bar x\). The quotient in stages through \(T_g/Z\) makes it \(\operatorname{vol}(T_g/Z)O_g(c_u)=O_g(c_u)\), with exactly the torus normalization fixed above. Formula (6.31) now proves (6.48) and (6.47).

Two choices of \(u\) give locally constant centrally covariant functions inducing the same distribution on this open set. They coincide: if their difference were nonzero at one point, choose a sufficiently small compact-open central quotient neighbourhood and multiply its indicator by the conjugate of that difference. This is an allowed inverse-central-character test, and its integral against the difference is strictly positive, a contradiction. Thus the formula is independent of the chosen smooth vector and represents the actual elliptic character. \(\square\)

For comparison, Casselman's Chapter II, §§7–8, discusses compact matrix coefficients, and Chapter III, §§3–5, discusses Jacquet coinvariants and positive Hecke operators. The proofs here give the division-matrix Gaussian factorization, its probability normalization, the nilpotence argument, proper orbital coordinates and the elliptic character integral. For a discrete series without compactly supported coefficients, the compact-conjugator bound in Theorem 6.21 is no longer supplied by this hypothesis. Section 6.15 proves absolute unipotent integration and vanishing constant terms, and reduces nonelliptic orbital convergence to elliptic estimates in the primary blocks. The remaining elliptic convergence estimate is still needed for this coefficient formula.

6.7. Jacquet modules and the full square-integrability criterion

Let \(G=\mathrm{GL}_r(D_0)\), \(d=\deg D_0\), and \(Z=F^\times\), over either kind of nonarchimedean local field. Put \(K_0=\mathrm{GL}_r(\mathcal O_{D_0})\) and \(K_m=1+M_r(\mathfrak p_{D_0}^m)\), \(m\ge1\). These principal groups are normal in \(K_0\) and form a neighbourhood basis. A compact-group average on a smooth representation is a finite linear combination on each vector. Thus the projection identities of Section 6.6 apply to smooth admissible representations without a unitary inner product as well.

Lemma 6.22 — the finite fixed space computes the Jacquet fixed space. Let \(V\) be smooth admissible, \(P=MN\) a standard parabolic, and \(a\) a strictly contracting central element of \(M\) as in (6.41). Write \(P_m\) for averaging on \(K_m\), \(T_a=P_m\pi(a)P_m\) on \(V^{K_m}\), and \(j:V\to V_N\) for unnormalized coinvariants. The stable kernel of \(T_a\) is \(V^{K_m}\cap V(N)\). For all sufficiently large \(L\), its stable image \(\operatorname{im}T_a^L\) maps isomorphically onto \((V_N)^{K_m\cap M}\). In particular the Jacquet module is admissible.

Proof: the kernel. Put \(K_+=K_m\cap N\). The same probability factorization as in (6.42), followed by moving the upper average across \(a^k\), gives, for \(v\in V^{K_m}\), \[ \begin{gathered} T_a^kv=\pi(a^k)E_{U_k}v,\\ U_k=a^{-k}K_+a^k. \end{gathered} \tag{6.49} \] The groups \(U_k\) are nested compact open subgroups of \(N\) with union \(N\): each upper block entry is expanded by a strictly positive power of the uniformizer. A vector belongs to \(V(N)\) if and only if \(E_{U_k}v=0\) for some \(k\). For the forward direction, express it as a finite sum \(\sum_i(\pi(n_i)w_i-w_i)\), put all \(n_i\) in one \(U_k\), and use right invariance of Haar probability there. Conversely, \(v-E_{U_k}v\) is a finite linear combination of differences of translates of \(v\). Thus \[ \bigcup_{k\ge1}\ker T_a^k =V^{K_m}\cap V(N). \tag{6.50} \] Finite dimension makes the kernels and images stationary. For a sufficiently large \(L\), the kernel of \(T_a^L\) and its image are complementary: an element of their intersection is \(T_a^Lw\) with \(T_a^{2L}w=0\), and stationarity makes it zero. Their dimensions add to the whole dimension. The operator is invertible on its stable image. It follows that \(j\) identifies that image with \(j(V^{K_m})\).

Proof: surjectivity. The image \(J_m=j(V^{K_m})\) lies in \((V_N)^{K_m\cap M}\). The relation \(j(T_av)=a\cdot j(v)\) from (6.43) makes \(J_m\) stable under \(a\). This action is injective, since it is induced by an invertible group element on coinvariants. On the finite-dimensional \(J_m\) it is therefore bijective.

Take \(u\in(V_N)^{K_m\cap M}\), lift it to \(V\), and average the lift over \(K_m\cap M\). Call the result \(v\). It is fixed by this compact group and by some sufficiently small \(K_l\): normality of \(K_l\) in \(K_0\) preserves that property under the preceding average. For large \(k\), \(a^{-k}(K_m\cap\overline N)a^k\subset K_l\). Consequently the lower and middle factors of \(P_m\) fix \(\pi(a^k)v\), and its upper factor disappears in coinvariants. Hence \(j(P_m\pi(a^k)v)=a^k u\), which belongs to \(J_m\). Invertibility of \(a\) on \(J_m\) now gives \(u\in J_m\). We have proved \[ \operatorname{im}T_a^L\ \xrightarrow[\ j\ ]{\ \sim\ } (V_N)^{K_m\cap M}. \tag{6.51} \] These compact subgroups form a neighbourhood basis in \(M\), so every compact-open fixed space of \(V_N\) is finite-dimensional. This proves admissibility. The same argument after conjugation covers every parabolic. \(\square\)

For the remaining assertions let \(\pi\) be irreducible admissible unitary with smooth unitary central character \(\omega\), and let \(V\) be its smooth vectors. Norms and absolute integrals are on \(G/Z\). Let \(q_F\) be the residue cardinality. For \(1\le j<r\), define \[ \begin{gathered} a_j=\operatorname{diag}(\varpi_F I_j,I_{r-j}),\qquad t_{\ell}=\prod_{j=1}^{r-1}a_j^{\ell_j},\quad \ell\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^{r-1},\\ P_j=M_jN_j,\qquad M_j=\mathrm{GL}_j(D_0)\times\mathrm{GL}_{r-j}(D_0). \end{gathered} \tag{6.52} \] Here \(P_j\) is upper block triangular. Its modulus at this element is \[ \delta_j=\delta_{P_j}(a_j) =q_F^{-d^2j(r-j)}. \tag{6.53} \] Indeed conjugation scales each of its \(j(r-j)\) upper division entries by \(\varpi_F\), and each entry has \(F\)-dimension \(d^2\).

Lemma 6.23 — Cartan coordinates, positive products and their volumes. For fixed \(m\), put \(T_c=P_m\pi(c)P_m\). If \(c,c'\) are diagonal field scalars with nonincreasing entry valuations, then \[ T_cT_{c'}=T_{cc'},\qquad T_{t_{\ell}}=\prod_{j=1}^{r-1}T_{a_j}^{\ell_j}. \tag{6.54} \] If \(\overline K_m\) is the image in \(G/Z\), the sets \(\overline K_m t_{\ell}\overline K_m\) for distinct \(\ell\) are disjoint and \[ \operatorname{vol}(\overline K_m t_{\ell}\overline K_m) =\operatorname{vol}(\overline K_m) \prod_j\delta_j^{-\ell_j}. \tag{6.55} \] There is a finite set \(B\) of diagonal division matrices such that \(G=\bigcup_{\ell,b\in B} ZK_0t_{\ell}bK_0\). More precisely, for any fixed \(m\) one can choose a finite set \(D\subset K_0\) with \[ G=\bigcup_{\ell,\ b\in B,\ k_1,k_2\in D} Zk_1bK_m t_{\ell}K_m k_2. \tag{6.56} \] The number of translates for each \(\ell\) is bounded independently of \(\ell\).

Proof: the matrix decomposition. Choose a division uniformizer \(\Pi\). Integral row and column operations diagonalize an invertible division matrix. Choose an entry of least valuation as a pivot; its ratios with all entries in its row and column are integral, so those operations eliminate that row and column. Repeat on the remaining invertible minor, and sort the resulting powers of \(\Pi\) by permutation matrices. Unit factors are absorbed in \(K_0\). Thus every matrix belongs to \(K_0\operatorname{diag}(\Pi^{h_1},\ldots,\Pi^{h_r})K_0\), with \(h_1\ge\cdots\ge h_r\).

These sorted integers are unique. If \(L=\mathcal O_{D_0}^r\), the lengths of \(L/(L\cap(gL)\Pi^s)\), for integral \(s\), are invariant under both integral basis changes. On the displayed diagonal they are \(\sum_i\max(h_i+s,0)\); successive differences recover the multiset of integers. Multiplying by a central scalar shifts every \(h_i\) by the same multiple of \(d\).

Write \(h_i=dq_i+\epsilon_i\), \(0\le\epsilon_i<d\). Since \(\Pi^d/\varpi_F\) is a division unit commuting with \(\Pi\), the diagonal units can again be absorbed in \(K_0\). The remaining diagonal is \(\operatorname{diag}(\varpi_F^{q_i})b\), where \(b=\operatorname{diag}(\Pi^{\epsilon_i})\) belongs to a finite set \(B\). The \(q_i\) are nonincreasing. Remove the common central power \(q_r\); the result is \(t_{\ell}\) with \(\ell_j=q_j-q_{j+1}\). This proves the asserted covering. It also proves the disjointness in (6.55), because these scalar diagonals have division valuations \(dq_i\) with smallest value zero, so no nonzero common central shift identifies two of them.

Proof: probabilities and volumes. Use the factorization of \(K_m\) for the minimal upper triangular parabolic. Every positive scalar diagonal contracts the upper factor, its inverse contracts the lower factor, and it commutes with the diagonal factor. The probability-average calculation of (6.42) applies to any pair \(c,c'\) in this cone, proving (6.54), including commutativity.

Uniqueness of the same Gaussian coordinates identifies \(K_m\cap tK_mt^{-1}\) with the product of \(tK_+t^{-1}\), the unchanged diagonal factor, and \(K_-\). The finite index is therefore the index of the contracted upper group. Its coordinate Haar Jacobian is the minimal-parabolic modulus \(\delta_B(t)\), so that index is \(\delta_B(t)^{-1}\). At \(t=t_{\ell}\), this is \(\prod_j\delta_j^{-\ell_j}\).

The same index computes the central quotient double coset. In fact, if \(k=ztk't^{-1}\), with \(k,k'\in K_m\), comparison of a diagonal entry gives \(z\in1+\mathfrak p_{D_0}^m\). Thus \(z\in Z\cap K_m\) and can be absorbed into \(k'\); passing to the central quotient has not enlarged the intersection. Counting the right compact-open cosets proves (6.55).

Finally choose a principal \(H\subset K_0\) contained in \(K_m\cap bK_mb^{-1}\) for every \(b\in B\), and choose representatives \(D\) of \(K_0/H\). If \(k_i\in D\) and \(h_i\in H\), then \(k_1h_1t_{\ell}bh_2k_2=k_1b(b^{-1}h_1b)t_{\ell}h_2k_2\), since \(b\) commutes with \(t_{\ell}\). This belongs to the indicated translate in (6.56). Every translate is contained in \(G\), and the preceding Cartan covering proves equality. All indexing sets except \(\ell\) are finite. \(\square\)

Theorem 6.24 — the complete Jacquet compactness criterion. For \(\pi\) as above, the following are equivalent: \[ \begin{gathered} \text{every smooth matrix coefficient is compact modulo }Z;\\ V_{N_j}=0\quad(1\le j<r);\\ V_N=0\quad\text{for every proper parabolic }MN. \end{gathered} \tag{6.57} \] One nonzero compact smooth coefficient already implies these conditions.

Proof. Proposition 6.19 proves the first-to-third implication and the assertion about one coefficient; the third implies the second. Suppose the second holds. Lemma 6.22 makes every \(T_{a_j}\) nilpotent on every finite \(V^{K_m}\). By (6.54), \(T_{t_{\ell}}=0\) as soon as any one \(\ell_j\) exceeds its nilpotence bound at this level.

For given smooth vectors \(u,v\), choose \(m_0\) fixing them, and then choose \(m\ge m_0\) large enough that \(bK_mb^{-1}\subset K_{m_0}\) for every \(b\in B\). For every \(k_1,k_2\in K_0\), both \(\pi(k_2)u\) and \(\pi(b^{-1}k_1^{-1})v\) are fixed by \(K_m\); here normality of the principal groups in \(K_0\) is used. On a translate in (6.56) their coefficient is consequently an entry of \(T_{t_{\ell}}\). It vanishes outside a finite box of \(\ell\)'s. The corresponding finite union of Cartan sets is compact modulo \(Z\). This proves compact support for every smooth pair. For \(r=1\) there are no proper parabolics and the Cartan covering is already compact modulo the centre, so the same conclusion holds. \(\square\)

Theorem 6.25 — the full \(L^p\) criterion. Fix \(1\le p<\infty\). All smooth matrix coefficients of \(\pi\) belong to \(L^p(G/Z)\) if and only if, for every \(m\ge1\) and every \(1\le j<r\), the finite-dimensional operator \[ S_{j,p}=\delta_j^{-1/p}T_{a_j} \quad\text{on }V^{K_m} \tag{6.58} \] has spectral radius strictly less than one. Equivalently, every eigenvalue \(\lambda\) of the normalized Jacquet action of \(a_j\) on \((V_{N_j})^{K_m\cap M_j}\) satisfies \[ |\lambda|\,\delta_j^{1/2-1/p}<1. \tag{6.59} \] An empty fixed space imposes no condition. This includes every matrix rank and division degree, in either field characteristic.

Proof: necessity, including the strict boundary. Choose an orthonormal basis of \(V^{K_m}\). Its coefficients are constant in absolute value on each central double coset in (6.55). Restricting their \(p\)-th-power integrals to these disjoint double cosets shows that, for every matrix entry, \(\sum_{\ell}\prod_j\delta_j^{-\ell_j}|(T_{t_{\ell}})_{ab}|^p<\infty\). Restrict further to one coordinate ray. Every entry of \(S_{j,p}^k\) is then \(p\)-summable in \(k\). If it had an eigenvalue \(\alpha\) with \(|\alpha|\ge1\), its operator norm would be at least \(|\alpha|^k\) on that eigenvector. In dimension \(h\), this norm is at most \(h\) times the largest matrix entry. The sum of the \(p\)-th powers of its entries could not be summable. Thus the spectral radius is strictly below one, including exclusion of the equality case.

Proof: sufficiency on the entire group. A finite matrix of spectral radius less than one has a bound \(\|S^k\|\le C\rho^k\) for some \(\rho<1\). To see the bound, on each Jordan block expand \((\alpha I+N)^k\) as the finite binomial sum in \(N\). A fixed polynomial in \(k\) times \(|\alpha|^k\) is bounded by \(C\rho^k\) after choosing \(|\alpha|<\rho<1\); a zero-eigenvalue block is eventually zero. The required finite Jordan decomposition follows from the elementary polynomial-module argument used in Lemma 6.9. Applying these bounds to all the commuting operators in (6.58) gives \[ \sum_{\ell\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^{r-1}} \left(\prod_j\delta_j^{-\ell_j}\right) \|T_{t_{\ell}}\|^p =\sum_{\ell}\left\|\prod_jS_{j,p}^{\ell_j}\right\|^p <\infty. \tag{6.60} \] Indeed submultiplicativity bounds it by a constant times a product of convergent geometric series.

For any smooth \(u,v\), choose \(m\) as in the proof of Theorem 6.24. On each translate in (6.56), the coefficient is constant in absolute value on the central \(K_m\)-double coset, and is bounded by \(\|u\|\|v\|\|T_{t_{\ell}}\|\). Left and right translation preserve quotient Haar measure, by unimodularity. Equations (6.55), (6.56) and (6.60), with only finitely many translates per \(\ell\), prove the \(L^p\) bound on all of \(G/Z\). Overlap between those translated sets only increases this upper bound. This proves sufficiency for arbitrary smooth vectors.

Finally Lemma 6.22 identifies the stable image of \(T_{a_j}\) with the Jacquet fixed space. Its stable kernel is nilpotent, so contributes only the eigenvalue zero. On the quotient, \(T_{a_j}\) acts as unnormalized \(a_j\), which is \(\delta_j^{1/2}\) times normalized Jacquet action. This gives exactly (6.59). \(\square\)

Corollary 6.26 — square integrability and the \(L^{2+\epsilon}\) boundary. In normalized Jacquet notation, square integrability modulo the centre and membership of all smooth coefficients in every \(L^{2+\epsilon}\), \(\epsilon>0\), are respectively \[ \begin{gathered} \text{Square integrability: }|\lambda|<1;\\ \text{Every }L^{2+\epsilon},\ \epsilon>0: |\lambda|\le1;\\ \text{for every }m\ge1,\quad1\le j<r. \end{gathered} \tag{6.61} \] For the first line, all Hilbert coefficients are square-integrable as well. One nonzero smooth coefficient satisfying an \(L^p\) condition with \(p\ge1\) already implies that condition for all smooth coefficients.

Proof. The first line is (6.59) at \(p=2\), and Theorem 6.15 passes from one nonzero square-integrable coefficient to all Hilbert coefficients. If \(|\lambda|\le1\), then for every \(p>2\) the factor \(\delta_j^{1/2-1/p}\) is strictly less than one, giving the second line by Theorem 6.25. Conversely, if (6.59) holds for every \(p>2\), let \(p\) decrease to two for each fixed eigenvalue; it gives \(|\lambda|\le1\). The smooth vectors are algebraically irreducible by Proposition 6.19. Every smooth pair is therefore obtained by finite linear combinations of left and right translates of any fixed nonzero smooth coefficient. These translations preserve \(L^p\), and \(L^p\) is a vector space for \(p\ge1\). This proves the last assertion. \(\square\)

For an explicit boundary calculation, take \(G=\mathrm{GL}_2(F)\) and the Steinberg unit edge vector of Proposition 2.12. Its coefficient has absolute value \(q^{-l}\) on the sphere with \(2q^l\) right edge-stabilizer cosets, each of volume \(2/(q+1)\). Thus, for \(p>1\), \[ \int_{G/Z}|\langle\mathrm{St}(g)v,v\rangle|^p\,d\bar g =\frac2{q+1}\left(1+2\sum_{l\ge1}q^{(1-p)l}\right) =\frac2{q+1}\left(1+ \frac{2q^{1-p}}{1-q^{1-p}}\right). \tag{6.62} \] At \(p=1\) this sum diverges; at \(p=2\) it is \(2/(q-1)\), as in (2.51). The algebraic cyclicity just proved makes every smooth Steinberg coefficient belong to every \(L^p\), \(p>1\). This example exhibits both an integrability boundary and square integrability with noncompact coefficient support.

Casselman's freely available 1995 draft, Theorem 4.4.6, gives the general reductive square-integrability criterion, and §§5.2–5.3 give the compact-coefficient criterion. The arguments above prove the full division-matrix version through Jacquet fixed spaces, explicit Cartan coordinates, exact quotient double-coset volumes and finite-dimensional power bounds. No character orthogonality or transfer-existence assertion is inferred from this coefficient criterion.

6.8. The canonical Jacquet pairing and full cone asymptotics

Let \(V\) be any smooth admissible complex representation of \(G=\mathrm{GL}_r(D_0)\), in either field characteristic. Write \(V^\vee\) for its smooth contragredient and use the bilinear evaluation pairing \(\langle v,\widetilde v\rangle=\widetilde v(v)\). Fix a standard parabolic \(P=MN\), with opposite \(\overline P=M\overline N\). The maps \(j_P:V\to V_N\) and \(j_{\overline P}:V^\vee\to(V^\vee)_{\overline N}\) first mean the underlying coinvariant quotients. We explicitly distinguish the unnormalized \(M\)-action from the normalized action \(\pi_P(m)=\delta_P(m)^{-1/2}\pi_N^{\mathrm{un}}(m)\). The modulus of the opposite parabolic is \(\delta_{\overline P}=\delta_P^{-1}\).

Lemma 6.27 — dual fixed spaces and common stable ranges. For each principal \(K_m\), evaluation identifies \[ (V^\vee)^{K_m}\simeq(V^{K_m})^*. \tag{6.63} \] In particular the contragredient is admissible. Choose a strictly contracting central scalar-block element \(a\) of \(M\), and set \(T_a=P_m\pi(a)P_m\) on \(U_m=V^{K_m}\). Its transpose is the positive operator for the opposite parabolic: \[ T_a^t=\widetilde P_m\pi^\vee(a^{-1})\widetilde P_m. \tag{6.64} \] Their stable ranges \(W_m\) and \(\widetilde W_m\) pair perfectly and identify with the two Jacquet fixed spaces by Lemma 6.22. These ranges are independent of the chosen strictly contracting central element.

Proof. A \(K_m\)-invariant functional is exactly a functional factoring through the average \(P_m\); conversely any functional on \(U_m\) composed with \(P_m\) is smooth and \(K_m\)-invariant. This proves (6.63). The compact subgroups are cofinal, so admissibility follows. Compact Haar probability is invariant under inversion, which makes the transpose of \(P_m\) equal to \(\widetilde P_m\). The definition of contragredient gives (6.64).

Let \(L_m=U_m\cap V(N)\). By Lemma 6.22 it is the stable kernel of \(T_a\), and \(U_m=L_m\oplus W_m\). The stable kernel of the transpose annihilates \(W_m\); its stable range is the annihilator of \(L_m\). Therefore evaluation restricts to a perfect pairing on \(W_m\times\widetilde W_m\), and Lemma 6.22 for \(\overline P\) gives \[ \begin{gathered} j_P:W_m\xrightarrow{\sim}(V_N)^{K_m\cap M},\\ j_{\overline P}:\widetilde W_m \xrightarrow{\sim}((V^\vee)_{\overline N})^{K_m\cap M}. \end{gathered} \tag{6.65} \] For another strictly contracting central \(b\), the probability calculation in (6.42) gives \(T_aT_b=T_{ab}=T_bT_a\). Thus \(T_b\) preserves \(L_m\) and \(W_m\). Its stable kernel is the same \(L_m\), so it is nilpotent there and injective, hence invertible, on \(W_m\). Its stable range is consequently \(W_m\). Applying this to the transpose proves the other assertion. \(\square\)

The inverses in (6.65) are denoted \(s_m\) and \(\widetilde s_m\). A lift may depend on \(m\). We next prove that its pairing gives a value independent of \(m\).

Lemma 6.28 — exact central-cone coefficients and independence of level. Put \[ B_{P,m}(u,\widetilde u) =\langle s_mu,\widetilde s_m\widetilde u\rangle. \tag{6.66} \] These pairings agree on the common domains at different principal levels and define a bilinear form \(B_P\) on \(V_N\times(V^\vee)_{\overline N}\). Let the Levi blocks have sizes \(r_1,\ldots,r_s\), put \(h_m=\max(1,\dim U_m)\), and take \(c=\operatorname{diag}(c_1I_{r_1},\ldots,c_sI_{r_s})\), \(c_i\in F^\times\). For \(v\in V^{K_m}\), \(\widetilde v\in(V^\vee)^{K_m}\), one has \[ \begin{gathered} v_F(c_i/c_{i+1})\ge h_m\quad(1\le i<s)\quad\Longrightarrow\\ \langle\pi(c)v,\widetilde v\rangle =B_P(c\cdot j_Pv,j_{\overline P}\widetilde v)\\ =\delta_P(c)^{1/2} B_P(\pi_P(c)j_Pv,j_{\overline P}\widetilde v). \end{gathered} \tag{6.67} \] The first action on the right is unnormalized. The threshold is uniform for the entire pair of finite fixed spaces.

Proof: the uniform range. Choose \(a\) with consecutive block valuations \(s-1,s-2,\ldots,0\). The nilpotent part of its finite operator is killed by the \(h_m\)-th power. If the displayed inequalities hold, \(b=a^{-h_m}c\) has nonincreasing block valuations. The probability identity gives \(T_c=T_a^{h_m}T_b\). The operators commute. On the common stable range, \(T_b\) is invertible: its Jacquet action is the invertible action of \(b\), and (6.65) identifies that action with its restriction. It follows that \(T_c\) kills \(L_m\), has image \(W_m\), and its action there is the canonical lift of unnormalized \(c\).

Pair with \(\widetilde v\). The stable-kernel component of \(v\) has been killed, and the stable-kernel component of \(\widetilde v\) annihilates \(W_m\). Removing these two components gives (6.67) with \(B_{P,m}\), first in unnormalized form and then with the displayed modulus. For \(P=G\) this is just ordinary evaluation, with trivial coinvariants and no inequalities.

Proof: comparing levels. If \(l\ge m\), the canonical lifts at level \(m\) are also \(K_l\)-fixed. Choose bases of the two Jacquet fixed spaces at level \(m\) and their level-\(m\) lifts. Apply the formula just proved at both levels to these finitely many pairs, with \(c=a^k\) and one sufficiently large \(k\). The two pairings agree on all \((a^ku,\widetilde u)\) from those bases. Since \(a^k\) acts invertibly on the first Jacquet fixed space, these vectors span that space as well. The pairings agree on their entire common finite domain. The union of these domains is the whole pair of smooth Jacquet modules, so \(B_P\) is well defined. This also proves (6.67) for that form. \(\square\)

Theorem 6.29 — the canonical pairing and opposite Jacquet duality. The form \(B_P\) is nondegenerate and \(M\)-invariant for unnormalized actions. It is characterized uniquely by (6.67) for sufficiently contracting central elements and arbitrary smooth lifts. In normalized notation it gives the natural isomorphism \[ (\pi_P)^\vee\simeq(\pi^\vee)_{\overline P}, \tag{6.68} \] where both Jacquet representations are normalized. The assertions hold for every parabolic, by transport under conjugation.

Proof: uniqueness and invariance. Any two forms with the coefficient property agree. Fix one finite pair of Jacquet fixed spaces, choose lifts of bases, and apply their coefficient properties at a common sufficiently large \(a^k\). Invertibility of \(a^k\) on the first finite space proves equality there, exactly as in the level comparison. The union of the finite domains proves uniqueness globally.

For \(m_0\in M\), the form \((u,\widetilde u)\mapsto B_P(m_0u,m_0\widetilde u)\) has the same coefficient property. Indeed apply (6.67) to \(\pi(m_0)v\) and \(\pi^\vee(m_0)\widetilde v\). A central \(c\) commutes with \(m_0\), so evaluation invariance makes the left side equal to \(\langle\pi(c)v,\widetilde v\rangle\). Uniqueness makes this transformed form equal to \(B_P\). This proves \(M\)-invariance without an assumption that \(m_0\) normalizes the chosen principal subgroup. The perfect pairings (6.65) prove nondegeneracy on the full modules.

Proof: the entire smooth dual. A vector of the opposite Jacquet module gives a smooth functional on \(V_N\): an open subgroup fixing it also fixes that functional, by invariance. Conversely let a smooth functional be fixed by \(K_m\cap M\). It factors through the compact average onto the finite fixed space. The perfect pairing there represents it by a unique vector of the opposite fixed space. Invariance under the same compact group makes the equality hold on every vector after averaging, proving surjectivity onto the smooth dual. Nondegeneracy gives injectivity.

Normalization multiplies the two unnormalized actions by \(\delta_P^{-1/2}\) and \(\delta_{\overline P}^{-1/2}=\delta_P^{1/2}\). Their factors cancel in the bilinear form, proving the normalized equivariance in (6.68). Conjugating a standard parabolic transports its coefficient property; uniqueness makes the resulting form independent of the conjugating choice. \(\square\)

Theorem 6.30 — the formula on every positive chamber face. Retain the vectors and \(h_m\) above. Let \(J\subset\{1,\ldots,r-1\}\) be the block boundaries of \(P\). For any positive scalar diagonal \(t=\operatorname{diag}(t_1,\ldots,t_r)\), suppose \[ \begin{gathered} v_F(t_i/t_{i+1})\ge0\quad\text{for every }i,\\ v_F(t_j/t_{j+1})\ge h_m\quad(j\in J). \end{gathered} \tag{6.69} \] Then the exact coefficient identity is \[ \langle\pi(t)v,\widetilde v\rangle =\delta_P(t)^{1/2} B_P(\pi_P(t)j_Pv,j_{\overline P}\widetilde v). \tag{6.70} \] Inside a Levi block the remaining gaps can be any nonnegative integers. Thus the assertion includes all the boundary directions of the full Cartan chamber.

Proof. Let \(a_P=\prod_{j\in J}a_j\), with the fundamental diagonals from (6.52). It is strictly contracting and central for this Levi. By (6.69), \(b=a_P^{-h_m}t\) is still positive in the full scalar chamber. Equation (6.54) gives \(T_t=T_{a_P}^{h_m}T_b\); both factors commute. Hence \(T_t\) takes \(U_m\) into the canonical range \(W_m\) and kills its Jacquet kernel.

The unipotent probability factors of \(P_m\) give a slightly different identity because \(t\) can vary inside the Levi blocks: \[ j_P(T_tv)=E_{K_m\cap M} \pi_N^{\mathrm{un}}(t)j_Pv. \tag{6.71} \] To verify it, use \(P_m=E_+E_ME_-\). The lower factor is absorbed across \(\pi(t)\) because \(t^{-1}K_-t\subset K_-\); the quotient removes the upper factor. The middle factor remains as precisely the displayed \(M\)-average. No commutation with this middle factor is asserted.

Pair \(T_tv\) with \(\widetilde v\) and replace its second vector by its canonical opposite lift, since the other component annihilates \(W_m\). Equation (6.71) then represents this pairing by \(B_P\). Its second Jacquet vector is \(K_m\cap M\)-fixed, so \(M\)-invariance removes the remaining average in the first variable. Finally replace unnormalized action by \(\delta_P(t)^{1/2}\pi_P(t)\). This proves (6.70). The case \(J=\varnothing\), \(P=G\), is ordinary evaluation. \(\square\)

Corollary 6.31 — finite exponential-polynomial expansions with every face included. Fix a smooth matrix coefficient and the scalar Cartan elements \(t_{\ell}\) of (6.52). There is an integer \(h\ge1\) such that the nonnegative chamber is a finite union of faces specified by \(\ell_j\ge h\) for \(j\in J\), with the other coordinates fixed in \(\{0,\ldots,h-1\}\). On each face, with its parabolic \(P_J\), the coefficient is exactly of the form \[ \delta_{P_J}(t_{\ell})^{1/2} \sum_{\alpha} Q_{\alpha}(\ell_J) \prod_{j\in J}\alpha_j^{\ell_j}. \tag{6.72} \] There are finitely many terms; every \(\alpha_j\ne0\); and \(Q_{\alpha}\) is a polynomial. These are finite-level Jacquet exponents for this coefficient. Together with the finite translated Cartan cover of Lemma 6.23, this gives finite families of such expansions covering the whole group modulo the centre.

Proof. Choose a principal level fixing both vectors and use \(h=h_m\). For each \(\ell\), take \(J=\{j:\ell_j\ge h\}\); the other coordinates have only finitely many possible values. Write \(t_{\ell}=t_Jt_b\), where \(t_J\) uses the unbounded coordinates and \(t_b\) the bounded ones. Theorem 6.30 applies. The finitely many vectors \(\pi_{P_J}(t_b)j_Pv\), together with the fixed second Jacquet vectors, have a common compact-open stabilizer in \(M_J\). Its fixed spaces are finite-dimensional by Lemma 6.22. The central elements \(a_j\), \(j\in J\), preserve them and act by commuting invertible operators.

Successively decompose into generalized eigenspaces for these operators; commutation preserves each previously obtained decomposition. On a resulting joint block write an operator as \(\alpha_j I+N_j\), where \(N_j\) is nilpotent. Its powers are \(\alpha_j^{\ell_j}\sum_{\nu=0}^{d_j-1}\binom{\ell_j}{\nu}\alpha_j^{-\nu}N_j^\nu\). Multiplying these finite sums and pairing the two vectors gives (6.72). Nilpotent parts therefore contribute polynomial factors and have not been discarded. For \(J=\varnothing\) there are only finitely many coefficient values. For the full group use the first Cartan covering in Lemma 6.23. A fixed smooth vector has finite \(K_0\)-orbit: its open stabilizer has finite index in the compact group. The finite division residues send those finitely many vectors to another finite set. Choose one principal subgroup fixing all these transformed pairs and apply the proof to that finite family. \(\square\)

Example 6.32 — the spherical coefficient with a repeated parameter. Take \(G=\mathrm{GL}_2(F)\), \(q=q_F\), and normalized \(\pi=I(1,1)\). Lesson 7, Theorems 4.2 and 7.1, proves irreducibility and unitarity. The compact induced model has a one-dimensional \(K_0\)-fixed space, represented by the function \(v\) equal to one on \(K_0\); use Haar probability there, so \(\|v\|=1\). Its central character is trivial. Put \(a=\operatorname{diag}(\varpi_F,1)\), and normalize \(\operatorname{vol}(K_0Z/Z)=1\).

The neighbour double coset has \(q+1\) right \(K_0\)-cosets, represented by \[ g_b=\begin{pmatrix}\varpi_F&b\\0&1\end{pmatrix} \quad(b\in\mathcal O/\varpi_F),\qquad g_{\infty}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&\varpi_F\end{pmatrix}. \tag{6.73} \] This is the list of index-one lattice neighbours from Section 2.3. Evaluating the normalized induced section at these upper triangular matrices gives \(q^{-1/2}\) at each \(g_b\) and \(q^{1/2}\) at \(g_{\infty}\). Thus the probability neighbour operator acts on the fixed line by \(\eta=2\sqrt q/(q+1)\).

The coefficient \(x_k=\langle\pi(a^k)v,v\rangle\) is radial on the tree. For \(k\ge1\), one neighbour is inward and \(q\) are outward, while all \(q+1\) neighbours of the base vertex have distance one. Consequently \[ \begin{gathered} x_0=1,\qquad x_1=\frac{2\sqrt q}{q+1},\\ qx_{k+1}+x_{k-1}=2\sqrt q\,x_k\quad(k\ge1). \end{gathered} \tag{6.74} \] Multiplying by \(q^{k/2}\) turns this into zero second differences. The initial values therefore give the exact spherical coefficient \[ x_k=q^{-k/2} \left(1+\frac{q-1}{q+1}k\right). \tag{6.75} \] It is positive. The vertex sphere has \((q+1)q^{k-1}\) right compact cosets for \(k\ge1\). Writing \(\beta=(q-1)/(q+1)>0\), its full coefficient integral, for \(1\le p<\infty\), is \[ \|x\|_p^p =1+\frac{q+1}{q} \sum_{k\ge1}q^{(1-p/2)k}(1+\beta k)^p. \tag{6.76} \] It converges exactly for \(p>2\). For \(p=2\) the summands grow quadratically; for \(p<2\) their exponential factor grows. For \(p>2\) the decaying geometric factor dominates the polynomial. Corollary 6.26 then gives every smooth coefficient in every \(L^{2+\epsilon}\), while (6.76) prevents square integrability.

The polynomial term also proves that normalized Jacquet action in this example has a nontrivial generalized eigenspace at eigenvalue one. Apply (6.67) to the fixed vector and its smooth unitary-dual functional. Since \(\delta_B(a^k)^{1/2}=q^{-k/2}\), it gives \[ B_B\bigl(\pi_B(a)^k j_Bv, j_{\overline B}\widetilde v\bigr)=1+\beta k \quad\text{for all sufficiently large }k. \tag{6.77} \] Let \(p(X)\) annihilate \(\pi_B(a)\) on the relevant finite Jacquet fixed space. Applying the corresponding shift polynomial to this eventual sequence gives \(p(1)(1+\beta k)+\beta p'(1)=0\). Because \(\beta\ne0\), both \(p(1)\) and \(p'(1)\) are zero. Its minimal polynomial therefore has a repeated root at one. Treating all repeated-parameter terms as semisimple would lose this coefficient and its correct boundary behaviour.

Casselman 1995, §§4.1–4.3, especially Proposition 4.2.3, Theorem 4.2.4, Corollary 4.2.5 and Theorem 4.3.3, gives a free source comparison for the canonical pairing and cone formula. The proofs here include level independence, the full smooth opposite dual, the modulus factors, every chamber face and its nilpotent polynomial terms.

6.9. Decay, matrix units and the discrete part of Plancherel

Fix a smooth unitary central character \(\omega\). In this section a discrete series means an irreducible admissible unitary representation with central character \(\omega\) and a nonzero square-integrable coefficient modulo \(Z\). Theorems 6.15 and 6.25 characterize precisely this condition. Inner products remain linear in their first variable.

Lemma 6.33 — an intrinsic length and a translation bound. Let \(g\in K_0\operatorname{diag}(\Pi^{h_1},\ldots,\Pi^{h_r})K_0\), with \(h_1\ge\cdots\ge h_r\), as in Lemma 6.23. Define \[ \begin{gathered} \sigma(g)=h_1-h_r,\\ D_C(g)=q_F^{-d\sum_{i<j}(h_i-h_j)}. \end{gathered} \tag{6.78} \] These functions descend to \(G/Z\), are invariant under inversion, and are locally constant. The sets \(\{\sigma\le R\}\) are compact and open in \(G/Z\). One has \(\sigma(gh)\le\sigma(g)+\sigma(h)\). For fixed \(a,b\in G\), there are constants \(C\ge1\), \(L\ge0\) such that, for every \(g\), \[ \begin{gathered} |\sigma(agb)-\sigma(g)|\le L,\\ C^{-1}D_C(g)\le D_C(agb)\le C D_C(g). \end{gathered} \tag{6.79} \]

Proof. Give \(D_0^r\) its maximum norm, using the multiplicative division norm with \(|\Pi|_{D_0}=q_{D_0}^{-1}\), where \(q_{D_0}=q_F^d\). Integral invertible matrices are isometries. Thus \(q_{D_0}^{\sigma(g)}=\|g\|\|g^{-1}\|\). Submultiplicativity of operator norms proves the length inequality; applying it also to \(g=a^{-1}(agb)b^{-1}\) proves the first bound in (6.79).

Here is a bound for every elementary divisor, which also controls \(D_C\). Put the singular numbers in decreasing order, so they are \(s_i(g)=q_{D_0}^{-h_{r+1-i}}\). They have the elementary minimax description \[ s_i(g)=\inf_{\dim_{D_0} W=r-i+1}\ \sup_{0\ne v\in W}\frac{\|gv\|}{\|v\|}. \tag{6.80} \] Reduce by the two integral isometries to a diagonal matrix with decreasing singular numbers. The span of its last \(r-i+1\) coordinates gives the upper bound \(s_i\). Every subspace of that dimension intersects the span of the first \(i\) coordinates nontrivially; any nonzero vector in the intersection has norm ratio at least \(s_i\). This proves the reverse bound and (6.80).

Choose an integer \(M\) with all four operator norms of \(a,a^{-1},b,b^{-1}\) at most \(q_{D_0}^{M}\). Multiplication on the left changes every ratio in (6.80) by factors between \(q_{D_0}^{-M}\) and \(q_{D_0}^M\). For multiplication on the right, replace \(W\) by \(bW\) and use the same comparison for the denominator. Consequently each sorted \(h_i(agb)\) differs from \(h_i(g)\) by at most \(2M\). Summing the resulting bounds for \(h_i-h_j\) proves the second assertion in (6.79).

A central multiplication shifts every \(h_i\) by the same integer multiple of \(d\); inversion reverses and negates them. This proves the stated invariances. Local constancy follows because each integral double coset is open. If \(\sigma\le R\), remove a common central shift so that \(0\le h_r<d\). There are then only finitely many possible sorted integer tuples, all in \([0,R+d-1]\). Their compact double cosets cover the indicated quotient set. Its complement is a union of the remaining open double cosets, so the set is also open and closed. \(\square\)

Theorem 6.34 — uniform exponential decay and weighted square integrability. For each discrete series \(\pi\) there is \(\eta_\pi>0\), independent of the smooth vectors, such that every smooth coefficient satisfies \[ |\langle\pi(g)u,v\rangle| \le C_{u,v}D_C(g)^{1/2}e^{-\eta_\pi\sigma(g)}. \tag{6.81} \] There is also \(\epsilon_\pi>0\), independent of \(u,v\), such that \[ \int_{G/Z}|\langle\pi(g)u,v\rangle|^2 e^{2\epsilon_\pi\sigma(g)}\,d\bar g<\infty. \tag{6.82} \] In particular, for a constant depending on the vectors, the unweighted squared tail over \(\sigma>R\) is at most \(C_{u,v}e^{-2\epsilon_\pi R}\). Every polynomial weight in \(\sigma\) is square-integrable against the squared coefficient as well.

Proof: one smooth pair. Suppose first \(r>1\), and choose a principal level as in the full covering argument of Theorem 6.25, fixing the finitely many rotations and division residues of one nonzero smooth pair. At this level every matrix \(S_j=\delta_j^{-1/2}T_{a_j}\) has spectral radius less than one. Choose \(\rho<1\) larger than all these radii. The finite Jordan expansion used in that theorem gives \(\|S_j^k\|\le C_j\rho^k\), after increasing \(\rho\) slightly if necessary. Equations (6.54)–(6.56) therefore bound the coefficient on every translated cone by \[ C\left(\prod_j\delta_j^{\ell_j/2}\right) \rho^{\sum_j\ell_j}. \tag{6.83} \] All the translating sets are finite. In the Cartan covering the residue matrix \(b=\operatorname{diag}(\Pi^{\epsilon_i})\), \(0\le\epsilon_i<d\), has bounded elementary divisors. For \(t_\ell\), \(\sigma(t_\ell)=d\sum_j\ell_j\) and \(D_C(t_\ell)=\prod_j\delta_j^{\ell_j}\). Lemma 6.33 shows that adding those fixed residues changes the length by a bounded amount and \(D_C\) by bounded factors. The compact rotations change neither. Equation (6.83) proves (6.81) for this pair with some \(\eta>0\).

For the integral, the volume factor in (6.55) cancels the squared modulus factor in (6.83). Since \(\sigma\le d\sum\ell_j+C\), choose \(\epsilon>0\) with \(\rho e^{\epsilon d}<1\). The weighted integral is bounded by a constant times \[ \sum_{\ell\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^{r-1}} (\rho e^{\epsilon d})^{2\sum_j\ell_j} =\bigl(1-\rho^2e^{2\epsilon d}\bigr)^{-(r-1)}. \tag{6.84} \] The translated covering may overlap; summing its integrals is an upper bound, which suffices. This proves (6.82) for this pair. If \(r=1\), the central quotient is compact and \(\sigma=0\), so both assertions hold directly.

Proof: the same exponents for all smooth pairs. Fix the preceding nonzero coefficient. Smooth algebraic irreducibility, proved in Proposition 6.19, expresses any other smooth coefficient as a finite linear combination of its left and right translates. The two weights in (6.81) are comparable under each fixed translation by (6.79). Thus the same \(\eta\) works for every such finite combination. For (6.82), translation changes \(\sigma\) by at most a constant and preserves quotient Haar measure; the same \(\epsilon\) works for each translate and their finite sums, by the finite Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. These choices give \(\eta_\pi,\epsilon_\pi\).

On \(\sigma>R\) the exponential weight is at least \(e^{2\epsilon_\pi R}\), giving the tail bound. Finally every polynomial is bounded on \([0,\infty)\) by a constant times \(e^{2\epsilon_\pi\sigma}\). This gives the last assertion. \(\square\)

The exponent is common to the representation; the constants need not be uniform over its smooth vectors. Equation (6.81) is a bound by the explicit Cartan weight in (6.78). Corollary 6.83 places these coefficients in the Schwartz algebra and proves rapid approximation by compact cutoffs in each Schwartz norm.

Let \(\mathscr H_{\omega^{-1}}\) be the Hilbert space of measurable functions with \(f(zg)=\omega(z)^{-1}f(g)\) and finite quotient squared norm. Write \(\mathscr D_\omega\) for its smooth compact-mod-centre test functions. Left translation is \(\lambda(h)f(g)=f(h^{-1}g)\). These tests are dense: in compact quotient charts, smoothness of \(\omega\) trivializes the central rule on sufficiently small compact-open cosets. Their successive finite partitions generate the Borel sets. Approximation of indicators in measure, then of simple functions in squared norm, proves density on a compact set; exhaustion by compact sets proves density in the whole space. The same argument gives a countable dense set, because \(G/Z\) is second countable.

For a discrete series and Hilbert vectors put \[ E^\pi_{u,v}(g)=d_\pi\langle u,\pi(g)v\rangle. \tag{6.85} \] These functions lie in \(\mathscr H_{\omega^{-1}}\). Denote their closed span by \(\mathscr C_\pi\).

Theorem 6.35 — the discrete Fourier blocks and Bessel inequality. The discrete series with fixed \(\omega\) form an at most countable set of equivalence classes. For \(f\in\mathscr D_\omega\), put \(A_\pi(f)=\int_{G/Z}f(g)\pi(g)\,d\bar g\). This map extends to every \(f\in\mathscr H_{\omega^{-1}}\), with Hilbert–Schmidt values, and \[ \sum_\pi d_\pi\|A_\pi(f)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2 \le\|f\|_2^2. \tag{6.86} \] The orthogonal projection onto \(\mathscr C_\pi\) is \[ P_\pi f=\sum_{i,j}(A_\pi(f))_{ij}E^\pi_{e_i,e_j}, \qquad \|P_\pi f\|_2^2=d_\pi\|A_\pi(f)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2, \tag{6.87} \] where \((e_i)\) is any orthonormal Hilbert basis. All sums in this formula converge in \(L^2\). The map \(u\otimes\overline v\mapsto d_\pi^{-1/2}E^\pi_{u,v}\) identifies \(H_\pi\widehat\otimes\overline{H_\pi}\) unitarily with \(\mathscr C_\pi\), with left translation acting as \(\pi\) on the first factor.

Proof. By Theorem 6.15, the functions \(d_\pi^{-1/2}E^\pi_{e_i,e_j}\), over all \(i,j\) and inequivalent \(\pi\), are orthonormal. Each coefficient space is nonzero. An orthonormal set in a separable Hilbert space is countable: distinct vectors have disjoint open balls of radius less than \(1/\sqrt2\), and every ball contains a different point of a countable dense set. Choosing one unit coefficient in each space proves the class-countability assertion. Admissibility and the increasing principal fixed spaces also give a countable smooth orthonormal basis of each \(H_\pi\).

For compact tests the Fourier coefficient along this orthonormal family is \[ \left\langle f,d_\pi^{-1/2}E^\pi_{e_i,e_j}\right\rangle_{L^2} =\sqrt{d_\pi}\,(A_\pi(f))_{ij}. \tag{6.88} \] For a finite orthonormal set, subtract its orthogonal projection from \(f\); expanding the nonnegative squared norm gives Bessel's inequality. Taking increasing finite subsets gives (6.86). It also makes the series (6.87) Cauchy and identifies its limit as the indicated orthogonal projection. Density extends these formulas to all \(L^2\) functions. More explicitly every entry is still the absolutely convergent scalar integral \(\int f(g)\langle\pi(g)e_j,e_i\rangle\,d\bar g\), by Cauchy–Schwarz and (6.29). Inequality (6.86) makes those entries a Hilbert–Schmidt operator, independently of basis. Applying (6.29) to finite tensor sums proves the asserted isometry; completing its domain gives precisely its closed coefficient span. The formula for left translation follows directly from (6.85). \(\square\)

For a compact open \(K\) with \(\omega|_{K\cap Z}=1\), put \(n_\pi(K)=\dim H_\pi^K\), and give \(\overline K=KZ/Z\) its quotient volume. The test supported on \(ZK\), of value \(1/\operatorname{vol}(\overline K)\) on \(K\) and extended by \(\omega^{-1}\), acts as the compact projection on every \(\pi\). Its squared norm is \(\operatorname{vol}(\overline K)^{-1}\). Consequently (6.86) gives the useful mass bound \[ \sum_\pi d_\pi n_\pi(K) \le\operatorname{vol}(\overline K)^{-1}. \tag{6.89} \] This does not require a lower bound for formal degrees.

Theorem 6.36 — absolutely convergent matrix units and the full discrete projection. For functions with the central rule above use \((f*h)(x)=\int_{G/Z}f(y)h(y^{-1}x)\,d\bar y\). For Hilbert vectors and discrete series with central character \(\omega\), every convolution of two functions (6.85) converges absolutely at every \(x\), and \[ \begin{gathered} E^\pi_{u,v}*E^\pi_{a,b} =\langle a,v\rangle E^\pi_{u,b},\\ E^\pi_{u,v}*E^\tau_{a,b}=0\quad(\pi\not\simeq\tau),\qquad (E^\pi_{u,v})^*=E^\pi_{v,u}, \end{gathered} \tag{6.90} \] where \(f^*(g)=\overline{f(g^{-1})}\). Left convolution by \(E^\pi_{u,v}\) extends to a bounded operator \(L^\pi_{u,v}\) on \(\mathscr H_{\omega^{-1}}\), of norm \(\|u\|\|v\|\). It acts on the first tensor factor of \(\mathscr C_\pi\) as \(w\mapsto\langle w,v\rangle u\) and annihilates its orthogonal complement. For an orthonormal basis, \[ P_\pi=\sum_i L^\pi_{e_i,e_i} \quad\text{strongly}. \tag{6.91} \] The orthogonal sum of these coefficient spaces is exactly the closed span of all irreducible admissible unitary subrepresentations of the left regular representation. Thus (6.86) is an equality on this discrete subspace; its orthogonal complement is the common kernel of all \(A_\pi\).

Proof: convolution and boundedness. Two \(L^2\) functions have an absolutely convergent convolution at each fixed \(x\), since its absolute integral is at most their two \(L^2\) norms by Cauchy–Schwarz and unimodularity. The integrand has trivial central covariance in the integration variable, so it is a quotient integral. In the first convolution of (6.90), the two factors inside it, after removing \(d_\pi^2\), are \(\overline{\langle\pi(y)v,u\rangle}\) and \(\langle\pi(y)a,\pi(x)b\rangle\). Schur orthogonality evaluates their integral as \(d_\pi^{-1}\langle a,v\rangle\langle u,\pi(x)b\rangle\). This proves the first identity; cross orthogonality proves the second. Inversion and conjugation give the adjoint identity directly.

For arbitrary \(f\in L^2\), a change of variable \(y=xz^{-1}\) gives the pointwise formula \[ E^\pi_{u,v}*f=E^\pi_{u,A_\pi(f)^*v}. \tag{6.92} \] Indeed the right side is \(d_\pi\int f(z)\langle u,\pi(xz^{-1})v\rangle\,d\bar z\); its scalar integral is absolutely convergent. Equation (6.29) and (6.86) give \(\|E^\pi_{u,v}*f\|_2\le\|u\|\|v\|\|f\|_2\). The matrix-unit formula shows that the bound is attained on the appropriate unit coefficient when both vectors are nonzero. Equation (6.87) shows that it vanishes on \(\mathscr C_\pi^\perp\). On \(\mathscr C_\pi\) it is the asserted tensor operator. Orthogonal partial sums of the rank-one projections on the first factor now prove (6.91). This also proves the operator adjoint assertions without exchanging any triple integral.

Proof: all discrete subrepresentations are included. Let \(T:H_\tau\to\mathscr H_{\omega^{-1}}\) be a nonzero bounded intertwiner for an irreducible admissible unitary \(\tau\). Average its output on the right over a sufficiently small principal \(K\), writing \(Q_KT\). Such averages converge strongly to the identity on \(L^2\), first for locally constant compact tests and then by density and their contraction bound. Some \(Q_KT\) is therefore nonzero. Evaluation at the identity after this average is a bounded functional: \[ |(Q_KTv)(1)|\le \operatorname{vol}(\overline K)^{-1/2}\|T\|\|v\|. \tag{6.93} \] This is just Cauchy–Schwarz on \(\overline K\), choosing \(K\) with trivial central character on \(K\cap Z\). By the Hilbert representation argument of Lemma 6.14 it equals \(\langle v,u_K\rangle\) for a Hilbert vector \(u_K\). Intertwining with left translation consequently gives \((Q_KTv)(g)=\langle v,\tau(g)u_K\rangle\). Right-\(K\)-invariance makes these averaged functions well defined on each open coset, so the identity also specifies their \(L^2\) representatives. Nonzero \(Q_KT\) supplies a nonzero square-integrable coefficient of \(\tau\). Theorem 6.15 makes \(\tau\) a discrete series. Each \(Q_KTv\) then belongs to \(\mathscr C_\tau\). Letting \(K\) decrease proves \(T(H_\tau)\subset\mathscr C_\tau\), since that space is closed. Conversely, the tensor description of Theorem 6.35 expresses \(\mathscr C_\pi\) as a Hilbert sum of copies of \(\pi\) under left translation. Both inclusions follow. The projection identities give the remaining norm and kernel assertions. \(\square\)

Corollary 6.37 — simultaneous compact trace approximation. Choose a smooth unit vector \(u\in H_\pi^K\), and set \[ E=E^\pi_{u,u},\qquad E_R=\mathbf1_{\{\sigma\le R\}}E. \tag{6.94} \] These are smooth compact-mod-centre tests \(E_R\), all bi-\(K\)-invariant after taking \(K\subset K_0\). There are constants \(C,\epsilon>0\) such that \[ \begin{gathered} \|E_R-E\|_2^2\le Ce^{-2\epsilon R},\\ \sum_{\tau:\,n_\tau(K)>0} \frac{d_\tau}{n_\tau(K)} \left|\operatorname{tr}\tau(E_R) -\mathbf1_{\tau\simeq\pi}\right|^2 \le\|E_R-E\|_2^2. \end{gathered} \tag{6.95} \] The sum runs over all discrete series with central character \(\omega\). It is one bound for the entire family.

Proof. The compactness and local constancy assertions follow from Lemma 6.33 and smoothness of the coefficient. Theorem 6.34 gives its tail bound, including the factor \(d_\pi^2\). For a bi-\(K\) function, \(A_\tau(f)\) has both its domain and range compressed to \(H_\tau^K\). This follows first by the two compact averages for tests, and then for \(L^2\) functions by continuity of \(A_\tau\). Theorem 6.35 and (6.90) give \(A_\tau(E)=0\) for \(\tau\not\simeq\pi\), and \(A_\pi(E)w=\langle w,u\rangle u\), of trace one. For an operator on an \(n\)-dimensional space, Cauchy–Schwarz on its diagonal gives \(|\operatorname{tr}A|^2\le n\|A\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\). Apply it to each block of \(E_R-E\), multiply by \(d_\tau/n_\tau(K)\), and sum using (6.86). This is exactly (6.95). Blocks with \(n_\tau(K)=0\) vanish and require no division. \(\square\)

Example 6.38 — the Steinberg truncation with its exact error. Keep the unit edge vector and volume normalization of (6.62). Its formal degree is \(d=(q-1)/2\). Let \(E=d\,\overline{\langle\mathrm{St}(g)u,u\rangle}\), and, for an integer \(R\ge0\), truncate this time to edge distance \(l\le R\). The edge spheres used in (6.62) give \[ \|E-E_R\|_2^2 =d^2\frac4{q+1}\sum_{l>R}q^{-l} =\frac{q-1}{q+1}q^{-R}. \tag{6.96} \] These tests are bi-invariant under the Iwahori subgroup \(I\) fixing the two endpoints of the base edge. Its invariant space in Steinberg is one-dimensional. Indeed \(I\) has two orbits on the boundary: in the usual projective coordinate they are \(\mathcal O_F\) and its complement. Translations act transitively on the first; after inversion the second is \(\mathfrak p_F\), where the corresponding lower translations act transitively. The boundary function model modulo constants therefore has invariant dimension \(2-1=1\); compact averaging makes passing to this quotient exact. Consequently \[ \operatorname{tr}\mathrm{St}(E_R) =d\int_{l\le R}|\langle\mathrm{St}(g)u,u\rangle|^2d\bar g =1-\frac2{q+1}q^{-R}. \tag{6.97} \] The proof of (6.95) applies to this bi-\(I\) cutoff as well. For every other discrete series the trace tends to zero, with the simultaneous squared error in (6.95) bounded by the exact expression (6.96), taking \(K=I\). The volume \(2/(q+1)\) in the sphere computation is that of the full unoriented edge stabilizer; the smaller \(I\) fixes the vector and suffices for the trace compression. No invariance under an edge reversal is needed.

The discrete projection above concerns coefficient spaces in \(L^2(G/Z)\). Elliptic character orthogonality and completeness concern the different torus-weighted space (6.16). Passing from the first space to the second still requires orbital convergence and the corresponding character comparison.

6.10. Contracting elements and character descent

We use ordinary Haar measure on \(G\) in this section. For a smooth admissible complex representation \(V\), its trace distribution is \(D_V(f)=\operatorname{tr}\pi(f)\), \(f\in C_c^\infty(G)\). The operator has finite rank by the two compact averages in Proposition 6.16. A local character density on an open set means a locally constant function \(\Theta_V\) satisfying \(D_V(f)=\int f\Theta_V\) for every test supported there. The assertions below specify where such a density is proved to exist.

Lemma 6.39 — the slope parabolic and an adapted order. Every \(\gamma\in\mathrm{GL}_r(D_0)\) has a canonical decomposition of its right division vector space \[ W=\bigoplus_{i=1}^s W_i,\qquad \nu_1>\nu_2>\cdots>\nu_s, \tag{6.98} \] where all algebraic eigenvalues of \(\gamma_i=\gamma|_{W_i}\) have extended \(F\)-valuation \(\nu_i\). Let \(M=\prod_i\mathrm{GL}_{D_0}(W_i)\), and let \(P=MN\) be upper block triangular in this ordering. There is a compact open unital \(\mathcal O_F\)-order \(\mathfrak A\subset\operatorname{End}_{D_0}(W)\), with these block projections, such that, for \(K_m=1+\varpi_F^m\mathfrak A\), \(m\ge1\), \[ \begin{gathered} K_m=(K_m\cap N)(K_m\cap M)(K_m\cap\overline N),\\ \gamma(K_m\cap N)\gamma^{-1}\subset K_m\cap N,\\ \gamma(K_m\cap M)\gamma^{-1}=K_m\cap M,\\ \gamma^{-1}(K_m\cap\overline N)\gamma\subset K_m\cap\overline N. \end{gathered} \tag{6.99} \] Conjugation by positive powers of \(\gamma\) contracts \(N\), and negative powers contract \(\overline N\). These assertions remain true, with the same \(K_m\), for \(\gamma t\) whenever \(t\in1+\varpi_F\mathfrak A_M\), where \(\mathfrak A_M\) is its block diagonal part. All \(K_m\) are normal in \(\mathfrak A^\times\).

Moreover, \(s=1\) if and only if the cyclic subgroup generated by \(\gamma Z\) has compact closure in \(G/Z\). No semisimplicity or separability of \(\gamma\) is required.

Proof: the slopes. Factor the minimal polynomial of \(\gamma\) into powers of irreducible polynomials over \(F\). By Extensions of complete valued fields, Theorem 1.2, the absolute value extends uniquely to algebraic extensions, including inseparable ones. Every root of one irreducible polynomial therefore has the same valuation. The norm formula in that theorem makes this valuation rational. Group the primary factors by this value. Bézout identities for the pairwise coprime grouped polynomials give their projection idempotents as polynomials in \(\gamma\): the identities give the direct sum of their kernels, and each projection is identity on its kernel and zero on the others. These are right-\(D_0\)-linear idempotents, proving (6.98). This also proves uniqueness of the decomposition.

Choose \(e\ge1\) with \(a_i=e\nu_i\in\mathbb Z\), and put \(u_i=\varpi_F^{-a_i}\gamma_i^e\). All roots of the ordinary \(F\)-linear characteristic polynomial of \(u_i\) are units. Its coefficients are integral and its constant coefficient is a unit. Cayley–Hamilton consequently expresses both \(u_i\) and \(u_i^{-1}\) integrally in the finite span of its powers. Starting with any \(\mathcal O_F\)-lattice, its sum of these finitely many power translates is stable under \(u_i^{\pm1}\). Close it under right \(\mathcal O_{D_0}\)-multiplication, using a finite \(\mathcal O_F\)-basis of that ring. The result is a full division lattice \(L_i\) still stable under \(u_i^{\pm1}\), because the operator is division-linear.

Put \(L=\bigoplus L_i\), \(\mathfrak A_0=\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal O_{D_0}}(L)\), and \[ \mathfrak A=\bigcap_{j=0}^{e-1} \gamma^j\mathfrak A_0\gamma^{-j}. \tag{6.100} \] This finite intersection is a compact open unital order. Each block projection lies in every intersectand. On the upper block \(\operatorname{Hom}_{D_0}(W_j,W_i)\), \(i<j\), conjugation by \(\gamma^e\) takes its \(\mathfrak A_0\)-lattice onto \(\varpi_F^{a_i-a_j}\) times that lattice. Here the \(u_i\) preserve \(L_i\), and \(a_i-a_j\ge1\). The inverse action has the corresponding contracting property on lower blocks. On diagonal blocks conjugation by \(\gamma^e\) preserves \(\mathfrak A_0\).

Intersecting as in (6.100) shows that one step preserves the upper lattice, its inverse preserves the lower lattice, and one step preserves the diagonal order in both directions. For example the upper intersectands after one step have indices \(1,\ldots,e\); the last is contained in the missing index-zero lattice. For the lower assertion use its inverse step: the index-minus-one intersectand is contained in the missing index-\(e-1\) lattice, since the \(e\)-th positive step expands the lower lattice. The \(e\)-th contracting steps take the resulting upper and lower lattices into \(\varpi_F\) times themselves. This proves contraction and cofinality.

Block Gaussian elimination in \(1+\varpi_F^m\mathfrak A\) gives the factorization (6.99). Pivots \(1+\varpi_F^m X\) have inverses by their convergent geometric series in the order; Schur complements stay in their block lattices because \(\mathfrak A\) is closed under multiplication. Induction on the blocks proves existence and uniqueness. The same argument in each finite quotient modulo \(\varpi_F^l\mathfrak A\) proves that the product of the three Haar probabilities is the Haar probability of \(K_m\). Equivalently, one can count the unique Gaussian coordinates in that finite quotient and then pass to the inverse limit. Normality in \(\mathfrak A^\times\) follows directly from the two-sided ideal \(\varpi_F^m\mathfrak A\).

A block diagonal \(t\in1+\varpi_F\mathfrak A_M\) normalizes the order lattices, and its conjugation is identity modulo \(\varpi_F\) on them. Thus the upper action of \(\gamma t\) has the same reduction as that of \(\gamma\), whose \(e\)-th power is zero there. Its \(e\)-th power also contracts by at least \(\varpi_F\). The inverse lower action has the same property; the middle order remains normalized. This proves all assertions for these perturbations.

If all slopes coincide, \(\gamma^e=\varpi_F^{a_1}u\) for an integral lattice automorphism \(u\). The powers of \(\gamma\) modulo \(Z\) lie in a finite union of translates of \(\operatorname{GL}_{\mathcal O_{D_0}}(L)\), so their closure is compact. If two slopes differ, conjugation by \(\gamma\) on the corresponding off-diagonal homomorphisms has eigenvalues with nonzero valuation. Indeed after splitting the division algebra and triangularizing the matrices, those eigenvalues are the ratios of their eigenvalues on the two blocks. Positive or negative powers of this operator are unbounded. A compact closure of the powers in \(G/Z\) would have bounded image under the continuous adjoint action, a contradiction. \(\square\)

In these coordinates \(P=P_\gamma\) is the slope parabolic. If \(r_i=\dim_{D_0}W_i\), its modulus is \[ \delta_P(\gamma) =q_F^{-d^2\sum_{i<j}r_i r_j(\nu_i-\nu_j)}. \tag{6.101} \] To prove this, conjugation on the \(i,j\) block has \(F\)-dimension \(d^2r_i r_j\), and all its eigenvalues have valuation \(\nu_i-\nu_j\). Taking the determinant and multiplying the block factors gives exactly (6.101).

Lemma 6.40 — finite generation of Jacquet modules. If \(V\) is generated by finitely many smooth vectors, then \(V_N\) is finitely generated as an \(M\)-representation. If \(V\) is also admissible and every block of \(M\) has division dimension one, then \(V_N\) is finite-dimensional.

Proof. In a division basis, \(G=PK_0\). Here is a direct Iwasawa proof. In the last row of a matrix, move an entry of least division valuation to the last position. Its left ratios with the other row entries are integral. Integral column operations therefore kill those other entries. The remaining upper-left matrix is invertible; repeat on it. Right multiplication by the resulting integral invertible matrix makes the original matrix upper triangular. This proves \(G=BK_0\), hence \(G=PK_0\).

The orbit of a smooth vector under compact \(K_0\) is finite, since its open stabilizer has finite index. For a finite generating set, take the finitely many such orbit vectors and project to \(V_N\). Iwasawa and the trivial action of \(N\) on coinvariants show that their \(M\)-translates span \(V_N\). This proves finite generation.

Admissibility of \(V_N\) was proved in Lemma 6.22. If \(M=(D_0^\times)^s\), the products of \(1+\mathfrak p_{D_0}^l\) form compact open subgroups normal in all of \(M\). Choose one fixing the finitely many generators just found. Normality makes it fix all their \(M\)-translates, hence the entire Jacquet module. Admissibility makes this fixed space finite-dimensional. \(\square\)

Theorem 6.41 — the exact contracting trace identity. Let \(V\) be any smooth admissible representation, \(\gamma\in G\), and use the parabolic and compact groups of Lemma 6.39. Write \(J=V_N\) with its unnormalized \(M\)-action \(\rho\), \(P_m\) for averaging on \(K_m\), and \(T=P_m\pi(\gamma)P_m\) on \(U_m=V^{K_m}\). Then the stable kernel is \(U_m\cap V(N)\), and the stable image maps isomorphically to \(J^{K_m\cap M}\). In particular, for every integer \(n\ge1\), \[ \begin{gathered} \operatorname{tr}_{U_m} T^n =\operatorname{tr}\bigl(\pi(\gamma^n)P_m\bigr)\\ =\operatorname{tr}\left( \rho(\gamma^n)|_{J^{K_m\cap M}}\right). \end{gathered} \tag{6.102} \] The same assertions hold for \(\gamma t\), \(t\in1+\varpi_F\mathfrak A_M\), with the same sequence of groups. The traces in (6.102) require no eventual-power restriction.

Proof: positive products and kernel. Put \(K_+=K_m\cap N\). For \(v\in U_m\), the middle and lower factors in the Gaussian average fix \(\pi(\gamma)v\), by (6.99). Thus \(Tv=E_{K_+}\pi(\gamma)v\). Since \(\gamma K_+\gamma^{-1}\subset K_+\), multiplying these probability operators gives \[ T^n v=E_{K_+}\pi(\gamma^n)v =\pi(\gamma^n)E_{\gamma^{-n}K_+\gamma^n}v. \tag{6.103} \] The expanded compact subgroups in the last average are nested and exhaust \(N\). The finite difference argument of Lemma 6.22, repeated here, says that a vector lies in \(V(N)\) exactly when one of these averages kills it. For a sum of differences, put all its finitely many unipotent translations in one expanded subgroup. Conversely the difference between a smooth vector and its compact average is a finite sum of such differences. Equation (6.103) therefore identifies the stable kernel as asserted.

Finite-dimensional stabilization splits \(U_m\) into that kernel and the stable image of \(T\), with \(T\) invertible on the latter. Projection to coinvariants identifies its image with \(j(U_m)\), because its kernel is exactly the stable kernel. Moreover \(j(Tv)=\rho(\gamma)j(v)\): the upper average disappears, and \(\gamma\) normalizes \(K_m\cap M\).

Proof: the entire fixed space. The finite image \(j(U_m)\) is stable under the invertible group action of \(\gamma\). Its restriction there is injective and hence bijective. Given \(w\in J^{K_m\cap M}\), average a smooth lift over this middle group. For large \(k\), \(\gamma^{-k}(K_m\cap\overline N)\gamma^k\) fixes that lift, by contraction and smoothness. The middle group also fixes its \(\gamma^k\)-translate, since \(\gamma\) normalizes it. Averaging that translate on \(K_m\) consequently has coinvariant image \(\rho(\gamma)^k w\). This belongs to \(j(U_m)\); bijectivity of \(\gamma\) on that finite image puts \(w\) there as well. This proves the stable-image isomorphism.

On the stable kernel, \(T\) is nilpotent, so every positive power has trace zero. On its stable image it is identified with \(\rho(\gamma)\). This proves the first-to-last equality in (6.102). Equation (6.103) also identifies \(T^n\) with \(P_m\pi(\gamma^n)P_m\) on \(U_m\). The finite-rank trace of \(\pi(\gamma^n)P_m\) equals the trace of this compression: choose a basis of \(U_m\) and extend it to the whole vector space. The diagonal coefficients outside its finite domain are zero. This proves the middle equality. Lemma 6.39 gives the same contraction and normalization for \(\gamma t\), so the proof applies to it unchanged. At power zero the two fixed-space dimensions may differ; no equality at that power is asserted. \(\square\)

Thus the normalized average of the trace distribution on the right coset \(\gamma K_m\) is exactly the normalized Jacquet trace at this level. We next prove when these averages define an actual locally constant character.

Theorem 6.42 — local descent from a Levi density. Suppose \(D_J\), for the unnormalized Jacquet module in Theorem 6.41, has a locally constant density \(\Theta_J\) in a neighbourhood of \(\gamma\) in \(M\). Then \(D_V\) has a locally constant density near \(\gamma\) in \(G\), and \[ \Theta_V(\gamma)=\Theta_J(\gamma) =\delta_P(\gamma)^{1/2} \Theta_{\pi_P}(\gamma), \tag{6.104} \] where the last character uses normalized Jacquet action. If \(P=G\), this is the identity assertion; it supplies no new character-regularity conclusion in that case.

Proof: a local conjugacy chart. Let \(\mathfrak s\) be the sum of the off-diagonal blocks. The map, for small \(X\in\mathfrak s\), \(Y\in\mathfrak m\), is \[ (X,Y)\longmapsto (1+X)^{-1}\gamma(1+Y)(1+X). \tag{6.105} \] After left translation by \(\gamma^{-1}\), its derivative at zero is \(Y+X-\gamma^{-1}X\gamma\). On each off-diagonal block every eigenvalue of \(\operatorname{Ad}(\gamma^{-1})\) has nonzero valuation, by the slope calculation. None is one. Thus this derivative is an invertible \(F\)-linear map from \(\mathfrak s\oplus\mathfrak m\) to the full algebra.

For completeness, the local inverse assertion follows by contraction and works in positive characteristic too. Compose a power-series map with its inverse linear derivative. On a sufficiently small complete lattice ball it has the form \(z\mapsto z+R(z)\), where the higher terms have Lipschitz constant less than one and map the ball into itself. For any target in that ball, the iteration \(z\mapsto y-R(z)\) converges to its unique preimage. Its successive differences shrink geometrically, and completeness proves both existence and uniqueness. Applied to the rational map (6.105), whose matrix inverse is a convergent geometric series near zero, this gives an open image containing \(\gamma\).

Shrink these balls so that \(1+X\in\mathfrak A^\times\), and so that \(1+Y\) belongs to \(1+\varpi_F^a\mathfrak A_M\) for a chosen \(a\ge1\). Every \(x\) in some neighbourhood \(U\) of \(\gamma\) therefore has the form \(x=k^{-1}\gamma t k\), with \(k\in\mathfrak A^\times\) and \(t\in1+\varpi_F^a\mathfrak A_M\). All these \(k\) normalize every \(K_m\).

Proof: one common partition, not a limit of unrelated averages. Choose \(a\) large enough that \(\Theta_J\) is constant, of value \(c\), on \(\gamma(1+\varpi_F^a\mathfrak A_M)\). For every \(m\ge a\) and every such \(t\), Theorem 6.41 and the defining density property on \(M\) give \[ \frac{D_V(\mathbf1_{\gamma tK_m})}{\operatorname{vol}_G(K_m)} =\frac{D_J(\mathbf1_{\gamma t(K_m\cap M)})} {\operatorname{vol}_M(K_m\cap M)}=c. \tag{6.106} \] Conjugation invariance of the finite-rank trace, and normalization of \(K_m\) by \(k\), give the same first average on \(xK_m\). Conjugation by this compact-group element preserves Haar measure. Shrink \(U\) to \(\gamma K_b\) contained in the chart. For every sufficiently large \(m\), its right-\(K_m\) cosets thus all have average \(c\).

Any smooth compact test supported in this \(U\) is, for one sufficiently large \(m\), a finite linear combination of indicators of those right cosets. Compactness and local constancy give that common level. Equation (6.106) then gives \(D_V(f)=c\int f\). This proves an actual locally constant density on \(U\), without assuming regularity of \(D_V\). Normalization twists the Levi action by \(\delta_P^{-1/2}\). This character is locally constant and equals its value at \(\gamma\) on a sufficiently small neighbourhood, giving the second equality in (6.104). \(\square\)

Corollary 6.43 — support for zero proper Jacquet modules. Suppose \(V_N=0\) for every proper parabolic. Its trace distribution vanishes on the open set of elements whose cyclic subgroup modulo \(Z\) does not have compact closure. This includes nonsemisimple elements and, in positive characteristic, elements with inseparable primary factors.

Proof. For every such \(\gamma\), Lemma 6.39 gives a proper \(P_\gamma\). Its Jacquet module is zero, whose trace distribution has zero density everywhere. Theorem 6.42 proves that \(D_V\) vanishes on a neighbourhood of \(\gamma\). These neighbourhoods cover the indicated open set, and a compactly supported locally constant test has a finite compact-open partition subordinate to them, so its trace is zero. In the unitary irreducible category Theorem 6.24 makes the hypothesis equivalent to compact support modulo the centre of all smooth coefficients. The conclusion concerns the trace distribution; it asserts no vanishing on compact split regular elements. \(\square\)

Corollary 6.44 — the minimal slope-Levi character is an ordinary finite trace. Suppose every \(W_i\) in (6.98) has division dimension one, and \(V\) is finitely generated and admissible. Then \(D_V\) has a locally constant density near \(\gamma\), with \[ \Theta_V(\gamma) =\operatorname{tr}(\rho(\gamma)|_{V_N}). \tag{6.107} \] The same conclusion holds whenever this Jacquet module is known directly to be finite-dimensional.

Proof. Lemma 6.40 gives finite dimension. A finite-dimensional smooth representation is trivial on some common open subgroup: intersect the stabilizers of a basis. Hence \(m\mapsto\operatorname{tr}\rho(m)\) is locally constant everywhere and is its trace density, by integrating the finite matrix entries. Theorem 6.42 applies and gives (6.107). \(\square\)

Example 6.45 — principal series and Steinberg on all noncompact split classes of \(\mathrm{GL}_2(F)\). Write \(\gamma=\operatorname{diag}(a,b)\), \(0<|a/b|<1\). The two-cell calculation in Lesson 6, Theorem 4.1, gives the two normalized Jacquet constituents of \(I(\mu_1,\mu_2)\), without assuming that the extension splits. Trace is additive on an invariant subspace and its quotient. Thus Corollary 6.44 gives the actual character value \[ \Theta_{I(\mu_1,\mu_2)}(\gamma) =|a/b|^{1/2} \bigl(\mu_1(a)\mu_2(b)+\mu_2(a)\mu_1(b)\bigr). \tag{6.108} \] For equal parameters \(\mu_1=\mu_2=1\), this is \(2|a/b|^{1/2}\). At \(\operatorname{diag}(\varpi_F^k,1)\), \(k\ge1\), it is \(2q_F^{-k/2}\), whereas the spherical coefficient of (6.75) has its nonzero linear polynomial. Trace removes the nilpotent part; the coefficient need not do so.

For \(\tau=C^\infty(\mathbb P^1(F))=I(|\cdot|^{-1/2},|\cdot|^{1/2})\), constants and its Steinberg quotient give \(0\to\mathbf1\to\tau\to\mathrm{St}\to0\). The exact coinvariant argument in Lesson 6, Proposition 2.2, and its two-cell calculation make \(\tau_N\) have unnormalized constituents \(1,\delta_B\). Constants supply the first line, so \(\mathrm{St}_N=\delta_B\). Therefore \[ \Theta_{\mathrm{St}}(\gamma)=|a/b|, \qquad \Theta_{\mathrm{St}\otimes(\chi\circ\det)}(\gamma) =\chi(ab)|a/b|. \tag{6.109} \] For \(\tau\), (6.108) gives \(1+|a/b|\), agreeing with the sum of the constant and Steinberg characters. This also exhibits why Corollary 6.43 cannot be applied to every discrete series: Steinberg has a nonzero proper Jacquet module and a nonzero character here. Swapping \(a,b\) covers the opposite ordering. The formulas cover precisely the split regular classes with unequal absolute values.

Example 6.46 — a fractional slope, including characteristic two. Take \(D_0=F\) and \[ C=\begin{pmatrix}0&\varpi_F\\1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad \gamma=\operatorname{diag}(C,1)\in\mathrm{GL}_3(F). \tag{6.110} \] Its slope blocks have dimensions \(2,1\) and slopes \(1/2,0\). Here \(e=2\), \(C^2=\varpi_F I_2\), and (6.100) gives \[ \mathfrak A= \begin{pmatrix} \mathcal O_F&\varpi_F\mathcal O_F&\varpi_F\mathcal O_F\\ \mathcal O_F&\mathcal O_F&\mathcal O_F\\ \mathcal O_F&\mathcal O_F&\mathcal O_F \end{pmatrix}. \tag{6.111} \] Indeed conjugating an integral two-by-two block by \(C\) gives entries \((d,\varpi_F c;b/\varpi_F,a)\); intersection with the integral block yields its displayed order. The upper off-diagonal lattice is \(\varpi_F\mathcal O_F\oplus\mathcal O_F\). Multiplication by \(C\) preserves it and its square multiplies it by \(\varpi_F\). The lower lattice is \(\mathcal O_F^2\); its inverse-conjugation action is right multiplication by \(C\), with the same two-step contraction. On the upper radical the determinant is \(-\varpi_F\), so \(\delta_P(\gamma)=q_F^{-1}\), also obtained from (6.101). The trace identity (6.102) consequently uses the normalized factor \(q_F^{-1/2}\) at its first power, and \(q_F^{-n/2}\) at its \(n\)-th power. In characteristic two, \(X^2-\varpi_F\) is inseparable, but all the displayed lattice, probability and descent arguments still hold. No diagonalization, division by two or exponential map was used.

Casselman's freely accessible 1977 article, §§2–5, gives the contracting trace and character–Jacquet comparison. The slope-order construction and local descent above supply the matrix-group arguments used here. For a larger slope Levi, Theorem 6.42 retains its stated Levi-density hypothesis. Character densities in the remaining compact-mod-centre blocks, the orbital estimates and elliptic character completeness still require their own arguments.

6.11. Lattice enclosures and finite support projections

Let \(G=\mathrm{GL}_r(D)\), with the valuation conventions of Section 6.7. Thus \(D\) has degree \(d\), \(v_D(\Pi)=1\), and \(v_D(\varpi_F)=d\). All representations and averaging operators in this subsection have complex coefficients. We construct finite support projections in every rank. The final proposition concerns a finite alternating trace on the elliptic regular set; identifying that trace with the representation's character requires the additional exactness statement specified below.

Lemma 6.47 — lattice apartments and their enclosure. A vertex is a homothety class of full right \(\mathcal O_D\)-lattices in \(D^r\). A simplex is a chain \[ L_0\supsetneq L_1\supsetneq\cdots\supsetneq L_k \supsetneq L_0\Pi. \qquad L(a)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^r e_i\Pi^{a_i}\mathcal O_D, \quad a\in\mathbb Z^r/\mathbb Z(1,\ldots,1). \tag{6.112} \] The coordinate lattices for one \(D\)-basis form an apartment. A vertex \(x\) and a simplex \(\tau\) lie in a common apartment. Their enclosure \(H(x,\tau)\), defined as the intersection of all such apartments, is the following finite subcomplex of any one of them: \[ \begin{aligned} \min_{v\in\{x\}\cup\tau^0}(a_i^v-a_j^v)&\le b_i-b_j,\\ b_i-b_j&\le\max_{v\in\{x\}\cup\tau^0}(a_i^v-a_j^v) \quad(1\le i,j\le r). \end{aligned} \tag{6.113} \] Here \(\tau^0\) denotes the vertices. A linear automorphism preserving the actual lattices representing \(x\) and \(\tau\) fixes this enclosure pointwise. A full subcomplex is called convex here if it contains \(H(x,\tau)\) whenever it contains \(x\) and \(\tau\).

Proof. Lattice elementary divisors follow by the valuation elimination used in Section 6.7: choose a matrix entry of smallest valuation, clear its row and column with integral operations, and repeat on the smaller matrix. These operations work on either side over the division valuation ring. Consequently two lattices have simultaneous diagonal form. Apply this first to \(x\) and \(L_0\). The lattices \(x\Pi^n\) induce a finite filtration on \(L_0/L_0\Pi\); the chain representing \(\tau\) induces a flag on the same residue vector space. Two flags have a common adapted basis: successively choose bases of their intersection quotients, lift these choices, and extend within each step. To lift the resulting change of residue basis while preserving \(x\), order the elementary-divisor exponents. A residue entry forbidden by the filtration is zero; choose its lift zero. Lift the remaining block triangular entries integrally, with invertible diagonal blocks. This matrix and its inverse preserve both diagonal lattices. The lifted residue flag then diagonalizes every lattice in \(\tau\), proving the common-apartment assertion.

Normalize the coordinates of \(x\) to zero. Write \(a^0,\ldots,a^k\) for a chain representing \(\tau\); each coordinate stays fixed until it increases once by one. The relative profiles of these coordinates are intrinsic. Indeed, the residue dimensions \(\dim_{\kappa_D}((L_j\cap x\Pi^n+x\Pi^{n+1})/x\Pi^{n+1})\) are \(\#\{i:a_i^j\le n\}\). Their differences as \(j\) increases count the coordinates of each initial exponent that increase at each step. They therefore determine the multiplicities of all coordinate profiles. Bases for two common apartments can be relabelled to have the same profiles. The basis change \(h\) then preserves \(x\) and every actual lattice in \(\tau\).

Put \(c_{ij}=\max_{v\in\{x\}\cup\tau^0}(a_i^v-a_j^v)\). Preservation of these diagonal lattices gives \[ \begin{aligned} v_D(h_{ij}),\ v_D((h^{-1})_{ij})&\ge c_{ij},\\ b_i-b_j\le c_{ij}&\ \Longrightarrow\ hL(b)=L(b). \end{aligned} \tag{6.114} \] Thus every vertex satisfying (6.113) belongs to every common apartment, using the preceding alignment of bases. The same entry argument proves the asserted pointwise fixing.

Conversely, if \(b_i-b_j>c_{ij}\), the transvection \(u=1+E_{ij}\Pi^{c_{ij}}\) fixes \(x\) and \(\tau\). The lattice \(u^{-1}L(b)\) is not coordinate: its projection to \(e_iD\) has exponent \(\min(b_i,c_{ij}+b_j)<b_i\), whereas its intersection with that line has exponent \(b_i\). Projection and intersection agree for a coordinate lattice. Hence \(L(b)\) does not belong to the common apartment \(uA\). Exchanging \(i,j\) gives the other bound. The walls \(b_i-b_j=n\) subdivide an apartment into its simplices. Their half-apartments are subcomplexes, so the same description holds for simplices and their real realizations, not only vertices. After setting \(b_r=0\), the bounds allow finitely many integer vectors. This proves finiteness. The case \(r=1\) consists of a single vertex. \(\square\)

The local finiteness used below is also concrete. Simplices at a vertex are flags in the finite vector space \(L/L\Pi\). Every chamber is obtained from a full residue flag, and a lifted adapted basis carries it to the standard chain. Thus \(G\) has finitely many simplex orbits. Two coordinate vertices are adjacent precisely when the spread of their coordinate difference is at most one: one can then translate all its entries into \(\{0,1\}\). Pairwise adjacent vertices form a chain by ordering their corresponding residue subspaces.

Lemma 6.48 — compact averages and the face identity. Fix \(m\ge1\). For a vertex \(x=[L]\), put \[ \begin{aligned} U_x&=1+\varpi_F^m\operatorname{End}_{\mathcal O_D}(L),\\ e_x&=\frac{1_{U_x}}{\operatorname{vol}(U_x)},\\ U_\sigma&=\prod_{y\in\sigma^0}U_y,\qquad e_\sigma=\prod_{y\in\sigma^0}e_y. \end{aligned} \tag{6.115} \] Endomorphisms here are \(D\)-linear endomorphisms preserving \(L\). These definitions are independent of homothety and equivariant under \(G\). The vertex groups in a simplex mutually normalize, their product is a compact open subgroup, and \(e_\sigma\) is its probability average. In particular \(e_\sigma V=\bigcap_{y\in\sigma^0}V^{U_y}\). If \(z\) is adjacent to every vertex of \(\tau\) and \(z\in H(x,\tau)\), then \[ \begin{aligned} e_z e_\tau e_x&=e_\tau e_x,\\ e_x e_\tau e_z&=e_x e_\tau,\\ \tau\preceq\sigma\subset H(x,\tau) &\ \Longrightarrow\ e_xe_\sigma=e_xe_\tau. \end{aligned} \tag{6.116} \] The reverse-order version of the last identity holds as well.

Proof. A \(D\)-linear map preserves \(L\Pi^a\) exactly when it preserves \(L\), which proves homothety independence; geometric series prove that the displayed sets are groups.
